We approximately match their trainable parameter counts and keep the pretrained encoder fixed. Across two EEG foundation models and tasks covering emotion recognition, sleep staging, and EEG event classification, the native adapter shows no consistent advantage. For one backbone, differences between the designs are small relative to variation across runs. For the other, the token-wise control performs better on emotion recognition and EEG event classification. On sleep staging, the native adapter's advantage over the token-wise control is reduced when we also train the component that processes sequences of EEG segments. The relative performance of the two adapters depends on the backbone, task, and downstream training protocol. These findings motivate evaluating backbone-informed adapters against capacity-matched alternatives.
  </details>

- **[Meta-Frieren: Beyond Single Level Online Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07169)**  `arXiv:2611.07169`  
  _Vahan Arsenyan, Arnak Dalalyan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study a two-stage online optimization problem: at each round, a meta-learner first selects an agent-environment pair, then the chosen agent interacts with the environment, and receives a reward. We introduce Meta-Frieren, a general framework for maximizing cumulative reward in this setting. Our analysis yields a simple UCB-style criterion for meta-level selection and recovers prior guarantees as special cases while extending them to broader settings. We instantiate Meta-Frieren with GP-UCB under Bayesian and frequentist assumptions. We also derive, to our knowledge, the first high-probability regret bound for Feel-Good Thompson Sampling, specialize it to finite and parametric function classes, and integrate it into Meta-Frieren. Experiments support our theoretical results and demonstrate that Meta-Frieren can be successfully applied to hyperparameter optimization across multiple model classes.
  </details>

- **[Structural Conditional Independence Test via Neural Quantile Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07165)**  `arXiv:2611.07165`  
  _Khoa Nguyen, Bao Duong, Thin Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conditional independence testing is difficult when dependence is weak, localized, or expressed through distributional changes rather than a global mean shift. Existing tests usually return a scalar statistic or p-value, which summarizes evidence against independence but does not describe where the conditional law changes. We propose QRCIT, a quantile-resolution conditional independence test that compares P(X|Z) and P(X|Y,Z) through a quantile deformation learned by a shared model. A lightweight residual quantile-flow model first learns the baseline conditional quantile law and then learns the additional deformation induced by Y, yielding a global predictive-gain statistic with conditional-randomization calibration and an inspectable deformation field over context and quantile level. We also introduce the Structural Dependency Benchmark (SDB), which evaluates dependence structures motivated by real applications, such as context-local, tail-only, sign-reversing, moving-ridge, and mean-neutral patterns. Across synthetic post-nonlinear (PNL) and SDB benchmarks, QRCIT improves detection of structured alternatives, with empirical Type-I behavior evaluated across settings. Higher quantile resolution improves merged-SDB power at moderate sample sizes, while diagnostics restricted to known affected regions improve detection of localized dependence. On Sachs, METABRIC breast cancer, and CCLE/PLX4720, QRCIT provides competitive real-data performance and interpretable deformation patterns. These results support quantile-resolved conditional-law deformation as a practical framework for testing and diagnosing structured conditional dependence.
  </details>

- **[Depth or Description? When a One-Step Latent Score Replaces Lookahead in Proactive Information Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07155)**  `arXiv:2611.07155`  
  _Biswa Sengupta_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A proactive agent must decide what to ask before it acts. Exact lookahead values combinations of questions but is expensive and leans on a fitted model; a one-step score is cheap but can miss them. Our main finding: the need for depth often lies in how the hidden cause is described. A one-step score on the label can be blind to an interaction among answers. A score on a suitable latent cause is not, and where that latent encodes the decision it replaces most of depth at a fraction of the search. Described differently, the same problem can defeat the score while exact values do not move, so keep the latent small and check it against exact values. Probabilities learned from one language model's answers do not carry to another unless planning allows for both; those read from tokens are worth only a few labels.
  </details>

- **[Teaching Agents the "Principle", Not the "Reward"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07130)**  `arXiv:2611.07130`  
  _Shan Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has achieved strong performance in many tasks by letting agents learn from nothing more than a reward signal. However, in more complex tasks, agents still struggle to learn good, generalizable policies, even with carefully designed rewards and abundant samples. We argue that this difficulty stems from the reward decoding burden: designers encode their knowledge of the desired behavior into rewards and penalties, and agents must infer this knowledge from long sequences of noisy scalars. To address this burden, we propose prescriptive policy learning (PPL), which directly teaches agents higher-level knowledge about the tasks, termed principles of behavior, such as how to maintain balance, how to accumulate energy, and how to land smoothly. Through appropriately selected key quantities and references, PPL can directly indicate at every step whether the agent follows the principles and to what extent. Our experiments show that agents can learn good, generalizable policies without any reward. Taught only simple heuristic principles, PPL agents outperform reward-based methods with far fewer samples. Their policies also transfer directly to harder settings and keep performing well, demonstrating their strong generalization ability. In summary, PPL provides a new approach in which rough knowledge of the desired behavior is enough for agents to learn good, generalizable policies, even when the system dynamics cannot be accurately modeled.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Bayesian Gaussian Process Classification for Cancer Histopathology: Calibrated Uncertainty Under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07127)**  `arXiv:2611.07127`  
  _Yunru Zheng, Wun Ting Chan, Sampras Dsouza, Zhenyu Hong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable uncertainty quantification is essential for clinical deployment of cancer diagnosis models, yet neural networks and variational Gaussian process methods can produce overconfident predictions, particularly under the distribution shifts that arise when models trained at one hospital are applied at another. We present a scalable Bayesian Gaussian Process Classification (GPC) framework that operates on frozen pathology foundation model embeddings (Phikon) and combines accelerated Randomly Pivoted Cholesky (RPCholesky) for low-rank kernel factorization, Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) with non-centered parameterization for posterior sampling, and a pivots-as-inducing-points scheme for prediction. This makes HMC posterior sampling in the low-rank latent representation tractable at N approximately 300,000 training points, enabling HC-based low-rank GPC on substantially larger datasets than prior MCMC-based demonstrations. We evaluate on two histopathology benchmarks for breast cancer metastasis detection: PCam and CAMELYON17-WILDS, where the latter tests calibration under hospital-level distribution shift. Compared to stochastic variational GP and neural network baselines, our method improves calibration and predictive performance - reducing expected calibration error by up to 88% and false negative rate by up to 90% - with the largest gains appearing under distribution shift, suggesting that HMC-based posterior sampling provides more trustworthy uncertainty estimates for cross-site pathology classification.
  </details>

- **[Machine Learning for Live-Birth Prediction After IVF/ICSI From Structured Clinical Data: A Systematic Review, Meta-Analysis, and Critical Appraisal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07124)**  `arXiv:2611.07124`  
  _Abdul Raheem Shahzad, Muhammad Zaheer Khan, Umar Ajaib, Asad Ur Rehman, Juan Deng, Ming Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine-learning models predicting live birth after in vitro fertilisation (IVF) or intracytoplasmic sperm injection (ICSI) from routine clinical data report performance from near chance to near perfect. We systematically reviewed these models under PRISMA 2020, searching OpenAlex, Scopus and Web of Science to 22 October 2026, appraised them with PROBAST+AI and TRIPOD+AI, and pooled their discrimination with random-effects meta-analysis on the logit scale. We define what fixes the meaning of a model's output (decision point, endpoint, unit of analysis, data source) and map the 48 included studies by model family, decision point and validation design. Models evaluated only internally pooled to an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.814, against 0.756 (95% prediction interval 0.546-0.889) for models evaluated on a later period or another centre, and 0.764 for studies not at high risk of bias; neither difference was significant, and heterogeneity was extreme. Models whose decision point was not stated pooled to 0.910, the only significant moderator in meta-regression. Against logistic regression fitted to the same data, the best machine-learning model gained a pooled 0.056 AUC (95% CI 0.017-0.090). Of 48 studies, 47 were at high or unclear risk of bias, only four assessed the calibration slope, and no model has evidence of clinical impact. The certainty of the pooled evidence is low to very low; the gains that survive validation came from staging predictions to decision points and local calibration, not deeper architectures. We provide a TRIPOD+AI-mapped checklist, a benchmark specification and a research agenda.
  </details>

- **[A Night at the LLM Zoo: Data Conditioned Flow Matching for Generating Pretrained LLM Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07103)**  `arXiv:2611.07103`  
  _Scott Biggs, Bedionita Soro, Saumya Gupta, Bruno Andreis, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A challenge to Weight Space Learning as it expands from task specific and vision models to Large Language Models (LLMs) is the absence of a well curated and analyzed LLM population. To address this, we train such a model zoo for application to Weight Space Generation by systematically varying the data mixture composition between web text, books, mathematics, coding, and French across populations of 51M, 124M, and 355M parameter GPT-2 Models, each tier with count N = 100. We analyze these populations by cosine similarity, functional diversity, Leave-One-Mixture-Out modeling, and variance decomposition. We show that the zoo contains diverse models whose variation is structured by training mixture composition. We embed the training mixture composition to condition a Flow Matching model of complete pretrained LLM weights in Gram-PCA compressed weight space. By demonstrating zero-shot generalization to held out mixtures, measuring Continuous Ranked Probability Score skill, as well as finding minimal changes in downstream test-set perplexity and general capability benchmark scores, we show that our generator generalizes on the simplex of data mixtures to produce models that perform similarly to those in the zoo ex nihilo. Finally, we produce all source code and artifacts to support the continued expansion of Weight Space Learning and Generation into the LLM domain.
  </details>

- **[Shaping & Monitoring Models through their Internal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07071)**  `arXiv:2611.07071`  
  _Thomas Fel, Matthew Kowal, Matt Feiszli, Satchel Grant, Andrew Lee, Chris Earls, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Mechanistic Shaping, a unified framework for shaping models through their internal representations. Mechanistic Shaping is applied during training so that models follow specifications (rules) about which concepts they express, measured via their outputs or internal representations (e.g., no harmful content), and also which concepts they rely on, i.e., what they use to make their decisions (e.g., not a candidate's gender when screening a résumé). Across multiple domains (vision, medical imaging, language), scales (up to 32B parameters), and training paradigms (classification, SFT, DPO, GRPO), we find that Mechanistic Shaping substantially reduces targeted behaviors while preserving task performance. Since training against a representation can also modify it, we ask whether shaped concepts remain monitorable, a question that depends on the class of monitors considered. We therefore introduce a hierarchy of such classes, which separates a concept that escapes one probe from a concept that is truly gone. We characterize three geometric mechanisms of circumvention: the concept can drift away from the monitor, be erased from the layer, or persist in higher-order statistics as a nonlinear encoding, which we explain through a product-code construction. Although other mechanisms may arise in different settings or at larger scales, these three account for all of our observations, and each can be detected by a matching monitor. In practice, we find that shaped concepts largely remain monitorable: refitted linear probes recover the concept in most settings, and the original probe remains reliable whenever gradients do not flow through it. Taken together, these results point to the potential of using interpretability as a tool for training, and call for a correspondingly rigorous science of interpretability-based training.
  </details>

- **[Finite-Time Convergence and Regret Guarantees for Neural Reinforcement Learning in POMDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07069)**  `arXiv:2611.07069`  
  _Jingjuan Huang, Zhiyao Zhang, Alvaro Velasquez, Nathaniel D. Bastian, Jia Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) in Partially Observable Markov Decision Processes (POMDPs) is computationally challenging due to the curse of long-range dependence on history. Fortunately, recent studies have shown that a range of POMDPs that satisfy the filter stability assumption can be well approximated by a superstate Markov Decision Process (MDP) based on a truncated history in a finite-memory window, with a value gap that decays with the window length. However, existing finite-time convergence analyses on such superstate MDP surrogates are either limited to over-simplified linear function approximation or rely on an i.i.d. sampling oracle that is violated by POMDP trajectories, both of which severely limit the applicability of these theoretical results in practice. This motivates us to bridge the gap by investigating RL in POMDPs with nonlinear neural value function approximation. Our main contributions are threefold: i) We first establish a new finite-state MDP point-wise approximation error bound, which lays the foundation for our RL algorithm design and analysis in POMDPs; ii) By applying Temporal-Difference (TD) learning to POMDP-generated superstate transitions and targeting the Bellman fixed point of the superstate MDP, we prove a finite-time critic error bound with nonlinear neural value function approximation; and iii) By combining the neural critic function approximation error bound with the policy mirror descent framework, we obtain a finite-time sublinear regret against the optimal POMDP value. To the best of our knowledge, such a finite-time sublinear regret based on neural function approximation is the first in the literature for finite-memory POMDPs. Our numerical experiments also verify our theoretical results.
  </details>

- **[Robust Stream Classification using Time Neutralising Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06991)**  `arXiv:2611.06991`  
  _Brandon Gower-Winter, Georg Krempl_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data Streams are characterised as a potentially infinite source of data subject to non-stationarity. This non-stationarity, also called Concept Drift, often results in deployed predictive models having to be perpetually retrained as the underlying data generating process of the stream changes over time. Drift Detectors are a class of methods used to identify these changes, triggering these retraining procedures. Historically, Stream Learning has operated under the assumption that instances are only seen once, and cannot be stored for later use. Contemporary work relaxes this assumption, yet drift detection and simple resetting procedures are still ubiquitous.Building on related work which has shown that traditional Batch Learning algorithms often outperform Stream Learning algorithms when instances can be stored, we introduce Time Neutralising Trees (TNT), a Decision Tree architecture that enables robust stream classification in settings with Concept Drift. During training, TNT will filter out ("neutralise") old training samples that are no longer relevant given the current context of the stream. If those instances become relevant again, TNT will then re-include them. We evaluate TNT, and related algorithms on 24 real and semi-real classification data streams from the USP DS repository. Results show strong evidence that TNT achieves best-in-class performance on a wide-range of data streams with varying concept drift types.
  </details>

- **[Big Models, Small Gains: Frozen Forecasters as Classifiers on MONSTER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06988)**  `arXiv:2611.06988`  
  _Rishit Jain, Rishit Kar, Chetashri Bhadane, Nilesh Patil_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting foundation models are increasingly used as frozen feature extractors for time series classification, but it remains unclear whether their pretrained representations offer an advantage over strong classifiers that do not require pretraining. This raises an important question: do these representations provide useful information beyond what the model architecture alone can capture, particularly when labelled data are scarce? We present a controlled benchmarking of two forecasting models, Reverso and Chronos-Bolt, on six large datasets from the MONSTER benchmark, using 1%, 10%, and 100% of the available training labels. We compare their frozen representations against four established time series classification methods and random-weight versions of the same forecasting models. Our results show that pretraining improves classification accuracy over random-weight counterparts on five of the six datasets, with gains of up to 23 percentage points. However, the frozen representations do not reliably outperform the strongest non-pretrained baseline in any setting: they perform worse in 15 of 17 settings and tie in the remaining two. Further experiments show that restoring amplitude information recovers part of the performance gap, while fine-tuning and forecast-error features do not close it. These findings highlight the importance of strong task specific baselines and random weight controls when evaluating forecasting foundation models for classification. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Fixed Defaults to Calibrated Activation Policies for Long-Term Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06559)**  `arXiv:2611.06559`  
  _Shijie Xu, Boyan Tang, Kai He, Jianghua Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term time-series forecasting supports decisions in energy, transportation, and other applications where reliable predictions are needed beyond the observed data. Although forecasting research has advanced model architectures, fixed activation configurations provide limited guidance on how activation choices should vary across datasets and prediction horizons. This study aims to establish when task-specific activation choices improve forecasting accuracy and how previous experiments can guide the selection of suitable configurations for new tasks. We develop a calibrated selection policy that learns a reliable default from prior experiments, adapts the activation function and its location to each task, and retains the default when evidence for a change is insufficient. In an evaluation supported by 1,225 Informer training runs across 17 dataset variants and 51 forecasting tasks, the policy reduces mean squared error by 7.86% on average relative to the conventional fixed-activation baseline and improves 72.55% of tasks. These findings establish activation choice and placement as practical opportunities for improving existing forecasting models and show how accumulated experimental evidence can guide configuration decisions without repeating an exhaustive search for every task.
  </details>

- **[The Alignment Tax Is Graded: Task-Selective Capability Loss Under Length-Normalized DPO with SFT Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06476)**  `arXiv:2611.06476`  
  _Ye Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Setting. This is an empirical characterization study; we propose no new method and run no comparisons against alternative objectives. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B with sparse LoRA and length-normalized DPO plus an SFT retention term (the RPO objective) on structured financial-filing tasks, and track downstream capability as the implicit reward margin grows to $\sim 7$ $\beta$-scaled units (nf4 training; $\sim 3$ under fp16 measurement on the unquantized base). Question. Does margin growth degrade capability uniformly across tasks, or selectively? Findings. In a pre-registered study (8 seeds, 6 unseen at freeze) with artifact-aware scoring and TOST equivalence testing, the alignment tax is graded and task-selective: extraction is preserved ($\Delta = -0.55$ pts, equivalent at $\pm5$ pts), json loses little ($\Delta = -3.52$ pts, equivalence fails on a clustered item-level interval), and router degrades severely ($\Delta = -30$ pts). Router's loss concentrates in a margin 1-3 damage window containing a transient format-break spike. The margin is accounted for almost entirely ($\geq 96\%$) by suppression of rejected completions, with chosen reward approximately flat, consistent with the retention term anchoring it; we do not attribute the behavioral damage to any single gradient channel. Gold-logp deflation is task-selective (extraction $-0.15$, json $-1.04$, router $-1.47$ nats/token under fp16), with gold selectively protected relative to incorrect controls on extraction (8/8 seeds). QLoRA reward margins differ up to $3.4\times$ between nf4 training and unquantized fp16 measurement, with an axis-dependent gap. Scope. Length-normalized rewards with SFT retention, sparse LoRA on 8/32 layers, one 4B model, easily-separable synthetic preferences, three structured tasks. A no-retention control and a measured format-alignment metric are left to future work.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Asset Knowledge Transfer for Early Fault Detection of Data-Scarce Assets Without Target Fault Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06194)**  `arXiv:2611.06194`  
  _Zekai Wang, Yuxuan Li, Zhangyue Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliability management of industrial fleets depends on early fault warnings, yet lifecycle data across similar assets are highly imbalanced. A few assets accumulate run-to-failure histories, while newly installed or rarely failing assets provide only normal-condition data. This paper asks how diagnostic knowledge can be transferred from a data-rich source asset to a data-scarce target asset when no fault data have been observed on the target. We formulate cross-asset early fault detection as a zero-shot domain adaptation problem, in which zero-shot refers to the absence of target fault samples, and propose a framework that requires only normal data from the target. The framework comprises (1) a statistical process control step that labels the source asset run-to-failure data with an exponentially weighted moving average chart, reducing manual annotation to verifying flagged segments, (2) an Adapted Domain-Adversarial Neural Network (ADANN) that combines domain-adversarial training and a Maximum Mean Discrepancy penalty with an Adapted Classification-Domain (ACD) loss, which anchors the classifier normal region to the target own normal data, and (3) an ahead-of-time labeling scheme with a prediction horizon that targets incipient faults. Under an exact-classification assumption, a theoretical analysis characterizes the domain-adversarial term for a normal-only target and the alignment state in which source fault features remain separated from target normal features. On a public run-to-failure bearing dataset, ADANN attains the highest mean F1-score among four transfer baselines, with a nominally significant precision gain over the strongest baseline, and its robustness is examined under synthetic noise.
  </details>

- **[Neural Stochastic Forecasting for Lateral Molecular Diffusion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06157)**  `arXiv:2611.06157`  
  _Jacob Ede Levine, Mario Hidalgo-Soria, Chen Yun Wen, Yun Lyna Luo, Sai Chandra Kosaraju_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting the long-time diffusion of membrane proteins is challenging because molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are computationally expensive, while experimental single-particle tracking is often limited by finite observation times. Here, we introduce a neural stochastic (NS) model for forecasting the collective diffusion of membrane proteins from coarse-grained MD trajectories. The model combines a transformer-based representation of protein configurations, recurrent networks operating at multiple temporal scales, and a stochastic latent process with learned memory. A cross-attention decoder then generates correlated protein displacements while accounting for the instantaneous system geometry and periodic boundary conditions. We evaluate the model in two membrane systems: seven interacting PIEZO channels and a mixed system containing seven PIEZO and ten TREK channels. For the seven PIEZO system, the NS model reproduces the anomalous diffusion exponent measured in MD simulations $\alpha \sim 1$, substantially improving on a previously developed variational autoregressive model that exhibits superdiffusive behavior. The model also generates trajectories extending to 40,000 frames and captures the distinct diffusion characteristics of PIEZO and TREK populations in the mixed system. Comparisons of time-averaged mean-square displacements, Van Hove displacement distributions, and non-Gaussian parameters show that the model preserves key statistical features of the simulated dynamics. These results establish neural stochastic modeling as a promising approach for generating long trajectories of multi-protein membrane diffusion and extending the statistical analysis of molecular dynamics simulations.
  </details>

- **[Reward Budgeting Reduces Premature Convergence in Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05942)**  `arXiv:2611.05942`  
  _Mengni Jia, Mengyu Zhou, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL for LLM reasoning often reaches an early performance plateau as the policy distribution becomes increasingly sharp. Recent work has studied policy entropy as both a diagnostic and a control signal for this behavior. In our experiments, however, increasing entropy alone does not reliably change the eventual plateau. We analyze RL through its implicit target policy and derive a sharpness factor $\kappa$, together with an effective counterpart $\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}$, that describes how strongly reward or advantage gaps are translated into probability-ratio gaps. Motivated by this view, we introduce reward budgeting, a plug-in advantage construction that assigns a fixed total reward budget to each prompt. For binary rewards and independent on-policy rollouts, its unclipped score-function estimator follows the gradient of an exact finite-$G$ concave objective with diminishing returns, while its advantage gap decreases as more rollouts become correct. For general rewards, a shifted log-mean interpretation holds asymptotically under the stated regularity conditions. These results motivate, rather than establish exact objective equivalence for, the clipped implementations. On Qwen3-8B and Llama3.2-3B-Instruct, reward budgeting consistently improves a range of RL baselines, with gains up to +12.50 on AMC23 and +10.00 on AIME24 and AIME25. Overall, our results support an update-dynamics view of premature convergence and show that strong gains can be obtained without explicitly preserving entropy.
  </details>

- **[Unifying and Beyond the Theory of Pervasive Capability Degradation in LLM Post-training SFT from Feature Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05003)**  `arXiv:2611.05003`  
  _Hang Chen, Jiaying Zhu, Wenya Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pervasive Capability Degradation (PCD) refers to the phenomenon in which supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while adapting a large language model (LLM) to a target task, often erodes the general capabilities acquired during pre-training. Prevailing explanations attribute PCD to macroscopic distribution shift and therefore lack microscopic manipulability, leaving existing mitigation methods without a complete theoretical foundation. In this paper, we first develop a bottom-up geometric theory of PCD grounded in feature interference under superposition: narrow-domain SFT data disrupt the almost-orthogonal feature structure formed during pre-training, and the resulting interference drives activations across decision hyperplanes in the output Laguerre-Voronoi geometry, flipping predictions on general tasks. This theory unifies existing methods into six paradigms and reveals both the source of their efficacy and their limitations. We then argue that cross-entropy under one-hot supervision is structurally limited in resolving PCD on its own, and accordingly propose the AEGIS loss, which directly constrains the drift of activation margins along all competing token directions, thereby decoupling interference suppression from task adaptation. Across 3 model families and 14 SFT tasks, AEGIS outperforms representative methods of every paradigm on target tasks and pervasive capabilities simultaneously, at only about 1.3 times the computational cost of standard SFT. Its generality, scalability, and mechanistic interpretability offer a new perspective on mitigating PCD.
  </details>

- **[P$^2$-MOPD: Accelerating Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation via Parameter Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03953)**  `arXiv:2611.03953`  
  _Sinuo Wang, Jiacheng Lu, Zeyu Zhang, Maojia Song, Rui Sun, Beidi Luan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation consolidates expert capabilities by supervising student-generated trajectories, whereas task arithmetic offers a training-free alternative through linear parameter combinations. We view the linear combination of student and expert parameters as a single differentiable model and propose P$^2$-MOPD, which jointly optimizes the mixing coefficients and the student weights under the same on-policy distillation objective. Unlike task arithmetic, which stays within the span of the frozen student and expert parameters for any choice of merging coefficients, P$^2$-MOPD keeps the student trainable, so the experts act as parameter priors without confining it to this span. Across four domains at 1.7B and 4B scales, P$^2$-MOPD achieves faster and more balanced recovery than MOPD from the same initialization, surpassing its final average recovery in half the training steps. At the end of training, our 1.7B model reaches 94.7% average recovery, versus 80.9% for MOPD.
  </details>

- **[FADE: Factored Ability--Difficulty Filtering for Online RLVR Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03625)**  `arXiv:2611.03625`  
  _Tomas Duncun, Jonah Ellison, Mateo Ruiz, Hanae Fujita, Ingrid Solberg_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) learns only from prompts whose sampled completions disagree: all-pass and all-fail groups produce zero relative advantage and burn generation budget without moving the policy. Adaptive schedulers try to steer rollouts toward informative prompts, yet they rely on per-prompt pass-rate trackers that entangle two quantities evolving at very different speeds---the policy's shifting competence across reasoning skills and each prompt's intrinsic difficulty. When a single exponential average or Beta counter tracks both at once, short memory scrambles the relative ordering of prompts whereas long memory reports stale pass rates. We introduce FADE (Factored Ability--Difficulty Estimation), a bilinear state-space filter that separates the two timescales. FADE represents each prompt's success logit as the inner product of a whitened $K$-dimensional skill loading with a fast-drifting policy ability vector, minus a slow-shrinking scalar item intercept. Using Pólya--Gamma augmentation, each minibatch of binary rollout outcomes updates the $K$-dimensional ability posterior in closed form via a rank-$K$ Woodbury step, instantly transferring competence shifts to all unobserved prompts that share those skills while leaving item intercepts stable. From the resulting posterior mean and variance we derive a closed-form utility for the probability of drawing a non-degenerate rollout group. Across five reasoning benchmarks, two base models, and four scheduling paradigms under a matched rollout budget, FADE consistently improves downstream accuracy and calibration while adding less than $0.6$\,ms per step.
  </details>

- **[Unified dynamics of training, learning, inference, and reinforcement in neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01345)**  `arXiv:2611.01345`  
  _Mian Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Through nine nanoGPT experiments, we established training-learning-inference relations: realized updates elicit state-dependent finite-amplitude responses; learning persistently reorganizes distributed functional support, with target-specific readout-boundary crossings determining capability transitions; inference recruits learned support without changing the learned state. Factors identified in the fifth experiment yielded a direct predictor of post-update capability transitions, achieving 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% four-way macro recall across 5,088 targets. We tested these relations and prediction across five additional neural architectures. In a seventh system, concentrated correct feedback eroded previously mastered skills; rebalanced feedback restored their accuracy from 70.31% to 99.48%. Linking inference consequences to subsequent training closes these relations into unified training-learning-inference-reinforcement dynamics. We subsequently established a unified system of dynamical equations, used it to reconcile positive and negative findings across existing research, and derived mathematical relationships in other neural-network theories using only the assumptions of the original studies.
  </details>

- **[Profile-aware diagnostics of discarded parameter-Hessian mass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01335)**  `arXiv:2611.01335`  
  _Maxim Bolshim, Alexander Kugaevskikh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating block-diagonal curvature models involves two questions: how much curvature they omit, and what a specific correction changes at a finite step. We measure the squared off-block fraction of the parameter Hessian against references for group sizes and the observed within-group curvature profile. In CIFAR-100 ResNet-18 and a skip-free counterpart, similar initial discarded fractions accompany different profiles. At saved trained ResNet-18 weights, changing from stage to layer groups moves estimated off-block mass from below to above the profile envelope; an approximate conditional screen resolves this comparison in all six seed/input cells, while some skip free comparisons remain unresolved. Separately, in saved ResNet-50/Stanford Cars states, we compare candidates formed from off-group Hessian action, the remaining model residual and their combination through the same Hessian-informed map. At unit scale off-group input alone raises native minibatch loss relative to the base preconditioner candidate in 17 of 18 state/geometry comparisons, yet the combined candidate has lower loss than the remainder-only candidate in nine of those cases; preferences vary with scale and correction-norm matching. The profiles help assess block models and alternative groupings, while same-state candidate comparisons test a particular correction without equating structural mass with training gain.
  </details>

- **[Orbitol: Crystal Generation on Wyckoff Orbits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30086)**  `arXiv:2610.30086`  
  _Ivan Žugec, Joshua Rosenthal, Ivor Lončarić, Jan-Willem van de Meent, Tin Hadži Veljković_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models propose crystalline materials by learning composition and geometry, but unconditional sampling gives no direct control over the space group, which constrains whether responses such as piezoelectricity are symmetry-allowed. We present Orbitol, a crystal generator built on the Crystalite diffusion Transformer that replaces atom tokens with Wyckoff-orbit tokens. Given a space group and a collection of occupied Wyckoff positions, Orbitol generates the atom type and free coordinates of each orbit together with a compatible lattice, so every valid structure is invariant under the operations of the requested group. It generates structures two to three orders of magnitude faster than the symmetry-aware baselines at the evaluated settings. On MP-20, 13.8% of Orbitol's attempts yielded stable or metastable, unique and novel structures that retained the requested space group after relaxation, compared with 10.8% for SGFM, the next best model. We also introduce SPARC, a score that combines retention of the raw detected space group with agreement between the retained-group and training distributions. Finally, we used Orbitol to generate candidates in piezoelectric crystal classes. Of the structures selected by internal screening, 89% passed our density functional theory checks, and 79% also matched no Materials Project or Alexandria entry with the same space group. Their median figure of merit was nine times that of the piezoelectric materials computed in the Materials Project, and 24% of the new candidates ranked in that set's top 10%.
  </details>

- **[Are We Overthinking One-Step Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20331)**  `arXiv:2610.20331`  
  _Woobin Park, Jaeyeon Kim, Yilun Du, Sitan Chen, Bohyung Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and flow matching are now dominant frameworks for generative modeling in continuous domains, but they typically require multiple network evaluations at inference. This has motivated extensive research on one-step generative modeling primarily built on diffusion and flow, though existing approaches often rely on complex training machinery. In this work, we introduce Priming Models, a simple two-stage framework for training one-step generators. Motivated by the difficulty of distributional training from scratch, Priming Models are first trained on a reconstruction objective, e.g., pixel-space denoising at a fixed noise level, before undergoing feature-space distributional post-training. Our central scientific finding is that this simple reconstruction stage is already enough to entirely alleviate the difficulty of distributional training. Empirically, across class-conditional image generation, text-to-image generation, and world modeling, Priming Models significantly advance the Pareto frontier of training compute vs. generation quality. On ImageNet at $256 \times 256$ resolution, we achieve a state-of-the-art FID of $1.19$ among one-step generators using approximately one-tenth the training compute of prior methods with comparable FID. We open-source our codebase atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Refined Topology-Pass Scheduling: Damped Trust-Region Compensation for Dynamic k-NN Graphs in SE(3)-Equivariant Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19246)**  `arXiv:2610.19246`  
  _Jiankai Huang, Hanwen Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equivariant structure-prediction pipelines alternate coordinate updates with the construction of a k-nearest-neighbour (kNN) graph whose membership is a discrete, piecewise-constant function of the coordinates. Rebuilding that graph at every block boundary (PB) is the expensive part of an epoch, whereas reusing one frozen graph (F) is fast but leaves a topology gap. We study a two-pass design in which the cheap pass records a small amount of state (margins, kNN radii, measured per-boundary displacements) and a second pass compensates conservatively. We formulate the choice of the graph-construction basis as a damped fixed-point iteration whose unique fixed point is exactly PB, and derive (i) exact non-flip certificates with the two-sided constant $m_i/4$ and one-sided constant $m_i/2$, (ii) the minimum-variance (BLUE) replay gain $\gamma^*$ and the resulting feasibility crossover, (iii) an $\ell_\infty$ trust-region projection that preserves convergence, and (iv) an analytic near-tie spacing law $E[m] \approx r_k/(3k)$ that matches measurements to 1\%. Three compensation levels are implemented in C++ (L1: frozen set with live geometry; L2: margin-gated local repair; L3: damped, projected basis) and validated under a matched-conditions protocol (identical seed, MSA depth and trajectory family, only the topology mode differs). L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly ($D = 0.0000$ at every boundary), L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically: L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly when the trust region is removed, and L3 interpolates between the frozen and the PB topology. The recorded trajectory itself yields a measured $1.5\times$ Pass-1 and $1.76\times$ epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. An open-source implementation of the runtime and of all the experiments reported here is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Graph Metanetworks for Transformer Based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18471)**  `arXiv:2610.18471`  
  _Yonathan Wolanowsky, Guy Bar-Shalom, Fabrizio Frasca, Haggai Maron_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space learning treats the weights of neural networks as data, processed by other models called metanetworks. A key challenge is that different weights can realize the same function, related by a group of symmetries the metanetwork should respect. While symmetry-aware metanetworks for simple architectures such as MLPs are well developed, a comprehensive treatment of transformers, and in particular transformer-based language models, is still missing. Importantly, their weight space has a richer symmetry group: beyond neuron permutations, attention introduces continuous general-linear symmetries of the attention matrices and permutations of entire heads. Existing methods for transformer weights either capture only part of these symmetries or are restricted to a single, fixed architecture, and none accounts for language-model-specific components such as the token embeddings. Building on graph metanetworks, we propose a graph-based approach that is both symmetry-aware and applicable across depths and widths. We formalize a weight-space symmetry group of complete decoder-only language models and provide a graph construction that is invariant to it. We further introduce two new transformer-population datasets with novel tasks, such as training-corpus prediction and fine-tuning detection, on which our approach notably outperforms existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[Best-of-$N$ Guidance for Test-time Diffusion Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05108)**  `arXiv:2610.05108`  
  _Richard Lee Kim, Yeongmin Kim, Gyuwon Sim, Taekyu Kim, Minsang Park, Il-chul Moon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models achieve strong generative performance but often struggle to align generated samples with human preferences measured by a reward model. A simple yet effective algorithm for test-time alignment is Best-of-$N$ (BoN) sampling, which draws $N$ i.i.d. samples from a pre-trained diffusion model and outputs the single highest-reward sample. Despite its empirical success, BoN makes limited use of reward information, as it is incorporated only at the final selection stage without influencing the reverse diffusion trajectory during sampling. Consequently, BoN sampling does not improve the average alignment of generated samples and is primarily suited to single-output settings. We propose Best-of-$N$ Guidance (BoNG), a novel method that integrates the principle of BoN sampling directly into the reverse diffusion process. BoNG performs online BoN selection over denoising particles and adjusts the reverse diffusion process to steer the particle population toward higher-reward regions during generation. Specifically, by introducing an asymmetric guidance interaction among denoising particles, BoNG uses the current BoN particle as a guidance signal to the rest of the particle population. This particle-level interaction reshapes the sampling process toward higher-reward regions, enabling BoNG to improve not only the final best sample beyond Vanilla BoN sampling, but also the average quality of generated samples. Over 36 empirical comparisons, BoNG achieves the best performance in 29 cases, ranking first in 80.56% of the comparisons against SMC and Vanilla BoN sampling. BoNG also supports multi-output capability, achieving 1.3$\times$ ImageReward score of the latest sample-based guidance method with a 1.6$\times$ speedup. We release the code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fixed-point neural samplers on discrete spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01739)**  `arXiv:2610.01739`  
  _Jiajun He, Denis Blessing, Mouyang Cheng, Yuanqi Du, Carles Domingo-Enrich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sampling from discrete, unnormalized distributions without access to data is a challenging problem. Neural samplers offer a promising approach by training generative models from density evaluations directly. However, existing discrete neural samplers are prone to mode collapse, come without convergence guarantees when trained via fixed-point iterations, and are often tied to a specific reference process such as masked or uniform diffusion. In this work, we introduce Discrete Gibbs Iterative Neural Sampler, a fixed-point neural sampler that addresses these limitations, enabling efficient, scalable learning, substantially reducing mode collapse in practice. Besides masked diffusion, the framework also extends to transport between arbitrary distributions. DIG-IN mitigates mode collapse on Ising and Potts models and scales to amortized sampling with up to 2,500 sites, recovers alloy phase diagrams, and learns to sample biomolecular Boltzmann distributions from energy alone. Its transport formulation accurately estimates solvation and conformational free energies. As a maximum-entropy RL policy, DIG-IN outperforms the Gaussian, diffusion, and flow policies we compare against across 20 continuous-control tasks.
  </details>

- **[Learning Rate Transfer for Hybrid Transformer-SSM Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01172)**  `arXiv:2610.01172`  
  _Jimin Seo, Gyubok Lee, Yeonsik Jo, Kiwoong Yoo, Yeongoon Kim, Minhae Oh, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study learning rate (LR) scaling for hybrid architectures combining Transformer and State-Space Model (SSM) blocks, a class adopted by several recent production language models. In particular, we focus on the gap between the theoretical scaling rules derived for SSMs under zero-order-hold (ZOH) discretization at infinite width with growing state size, and the field-standard practical implementations using simplified-ZOH Mamba at fixed state size. Surprisingly, in this practical regime hybrid architectures achieve a near-zero LR transfer gap across widths 256-2048 and depths 4-32 up to billion-parameter scale using only the original $\mu$P prescription, even though SSM operations fall outside its Tensor Programs representability conditions and every parameterization we test fails the standard coordinate-check diagnostic of $\mu$P correctness. We attribute this to a two-condition decomposition of LR transfer in hybrid architectures: a global update-to-weight invariance, enforced by $\mu$P's initialization and LR scaling; and a local per-component balance, provided by AdamW's per-parameter normalization. Our observations show that the optimal LR is invariant to width up to 8$\times$, that this width invariance holds across depth, sequence length, batch size, and Transformer-to-SSM ratio, and that it transfers to Nemotron-H, a production hybrid outside our custom architecture set. We hope these findings fill the gap between theoretical scaling rules and practical hybrid implementations, and stimulate further research toward bridging it.
  </details>

- **[Manifold-Constrained Initial Noise Optimization for Efficient Generative Model Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00365)**  `arXiv:2610.00365`  
  _Jinho Chang, Jong Chul Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in distillation and flow-map models have enabled deterministic one- or few-step generation for high-quality data, facilitating a new branch of reward alignment approaches that directly optimize the initial noise from a Gaussian distribution. However, most existing initial-noise optimization methods rely on first-order gradient information, which is either inapplicable or suffers from instability and inefficiency in black-box reward scenarios. Here, we introduce ZeNOVA, a stable and efficient initial noise alignment method in a gradient-free manner. Specifically, we address existing algorithms' major challenge in black-box scenarios through annealed soft-value guidance, manifold-constrained hyperspherical Langevin dynamics, and Metropolis-Hastings jumping. Extensive experiments on image and video generative models show that ZeNOVA outperforms all evaluated zeroth-order baselines by optimizing the initial noise toward higher rewards substantially more stably while exploiting the geometry of the Gaussian prior, demonstrating its practical applicability to various black-box reward alignment.
  </details>

- **[Structure-aware Reinforcement Learning for Protein Directed Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39048)**  `arXiv:2609.39048`  
  _Zikun Nie, Suyuan Zhao, Yizhen Luo, Siqi Fan, Zaiqing Nie_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein optimization remains a longstanding goal in life sciences. Existing machine learning-assisted directed evolution (MLDE) methods primarily rely on sequence-only features, overlooking the critical spatial constraints and co-evolutionary interactions encoded in protein structures. However, directly integrating structural information remains challenging due to the scarcity of reliable mutant structures. To address these issues, we propose StructEvo, a novel structure-aware reinforcement learning framework for protein directed evolution. StructEvo employs a delta-structure fusion encoder to approximate mutant structure features via feature differences, enabling dynamic incorporation of spatial knowledge. The vast mutation space is then decomposed into manageable subspaces through a structure-aligned hierarchical action network, while a geometric constraint further stabilizes delta feature learning. Our approach outperforms prior state-of-the-art methods by 9.2% and 16.3% on two challenging optimization benchmarks, and further identifies an experimentally validated epistasis pattern in GFP, highlighting the importance of structural guidance for effective protein directed evolution.
  </details>

- **[Muon Sublates the Edge of Stability in LLM Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34915)**  `arXiv:2609.34915`  
  _Yanzhe Chen, Qifang Zhao, Xiaoxiao Xu, Fanghui Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon is increasingly used for language-model pretraining, yet its large-step dynamics are not captured by the classical edge-of-stability (EoS) picture of gradient descent (GD). In GD, loss neutrality, equal-magnitude update reversal, and marginal stability meet at a single learning-rate-dependent edge. We show that Muon breaks this coupling. For stochastic no-momentum Muon, we derive a coherence-corrected conditional loss-neutral boundary $2\rho_b/\eta$, while temporal alignment follows a separate geometry. Controlled experiments show that loss balance and temporal alignment respond differently to learning rate and batch size. Across our language model experiments, the 130M Llama-like LLM runs exhibit loss-boundary tracking with weak negative alignment, whereas the studied 1B LLM configuration shows stronger partial cancellation; in both settings, directions remain far from coherent reversal while training continues to improve. These results support a split EoS picture for Muon: a stochastic loss-neutral edge survives, but it is not accompanied by a universal temporal-direction signature. The source code for reproducing the experiments can be found inthis https URL
  </details>

- **[AnchorRep: Defending LLMs Against Cross-Model Adversarial Transfer via Representation Repulsion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32602)**  `arXiv:2609.32602`  
  _Gal Wertheizer, Rom Himelstein, Tomer Peretz, Avi Mendelson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adversarial attacks optimized on a single open-weight LLM can transfer to and jailbreak architecturally different models, allowing an attacker with white-box access to one model to compromise independently deployed systems. This creates a shared vulnerability across models, yet existing defenses are not designed for this cross-model threat. We find that cross-model transfer aligns with shared internal representation geometry, making it a natural defense target. AnchorRep targets this geometry directly with a lightweight LoRA adapter that pushes the defended model's internal representations of harmful prompts away from those of a frozen anchor model on the same prompts. Training uses a small set of harmful prompts and no adversarial examples. Across five models and four architectural families, AnchorRep reduces cross-model attack success rate to <=1.1% on 2,000 transferred attacks (0% on two), including the largest drop on Mistral (36% -> 1.1%). Existing defenses can reduce transfer, but only at high cost either inducing up to 77% degenerate benign output or increasing over-refusal by up to 18%. Because such degenerate benign outputs are not captured by standard refusal-based metrics, we introduce the Benign Garble Rate to quantify them. Our results suggest that cross-model robustness can be achieved by shaping representation geometry, without requiring attack-specific training
  </details>

- **[Peer-Grounded Counterfactual Path Planning for Chronic Health Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30838)**  `arXiv:2609.30838`  
  _Saman Khamesian, Hassan Ghasemzadeh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective behavioral intervention in chronic disease management requires a sequence of incremental steps, each grounded in what similar individuals have achieved. Multimodal records of daily behavior make such evidence available outside the clinic. Counterfactual explanation, an explainable AI approach, can turn these records into guidance, yet endpoint-only methods return a target state without a route to it. We propose POROS (Peer-Grounded Optimal Routes Over States), a domain-agnostic framework rooted in Bandura's self-efficacy theory and Festinger's social comparison theory. POROS constructs a Behavioral Progression Graph, a directed acyclic graph over observed patient states in which every edge requires both peer-grounded behavioral proximity and strict improvement in the observed health outcome. Every edge is bounded by a feasibility threshold derived from the cohort's day-to-day behavioral changes. Minimum-cost paths through this graph decompose behavioral gaps into incremental, peer-grounded steps. We evaluate POROS on two independent longitudinal cohorts of patients with type 1 diabetes, with a per-patient predictor estimating individual time in range (TIR, blood glucose within 70-180 mg/dL) along each path. Compared with an endpoint-only baseline, POROS returns multi-hop paths to the same target for 14.4% and 19.8% of days below the 70% TIR threshold, reducing the per-step change in carbohydrate intake, insulin dose, and bolus timing by 38-42% on the larger cohort. POROS also supports improvement within the same outcome class, connecting 78.1% and 83.7% of outcome-ordered pairs of well-controlled days. Together, these findings demonstrate how POROS translates multimodal health records into incremental, peer-grounded guidance for chronic disease management through observational path planning.
  </details>

- **[RLVR$^{2}$: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rubric-based Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23457)**  `arXiv:2609.23457`  
  _Hao Li, Zhengkun Zhang, Gangqiang Hu, Zhen Zhang, Yude Gao, Dai Dai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is expanding from tasks with well-defined correctness signals, such as mathematics and code, toward multifaceted quality requirements specified by multi-dimensional rubrics. Since policy optimization consumes one scalar per rollout, rubric-based pipelines must map multiple criterion scores into a scalar reward. This aggregation is often treated as score scaling, but it implicitly determines how quality dimensions trade off during training. The prevailing practice, normalizing each criterion and taking a linear combination, assumes that cardinal score differences are comparable across criteria and that gains on one criterion compensate for failures on another; both assumptions are unreliable when criteria are semantically heterogeneous. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rubric-based Ranking (RLVR$^2$), a verifiable ranking paradigm for rubric-based RLVR. For each criterion, RLVR$^2$ converts rubric scores into criterion-specific within-group ordinal outcomes, recovers a latent utility from the resulting comparison matrix, and merges these utilities into one training signal. By retaining only within-group ordering and discarding raw score magnitudes, RLVR$^2$ avoids calibrating heterogeneous rubric scales. It further supports objective-preserving attribute adjustment: auxiliary attributes that correlate with observed rankings but are not training objectives can enter the estimation without expanding the rubric or rewarding them directly. Across three model scales and 16 benchmarks, RLVR$^2$ consistently outperforms representative rubric-based baselines, achieving the best overall performance on most benchmarks at every scale. Analysis shows it controls systematic effects tied to reasoning efficiency and response formatting while preserving the quality objective.
  </details>

- **[The Token Before the Value Is the Key](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15545)**  `arXiv:2609.15545`  
  _Ke Cheng, Xin Xu, Zhichao Chen, Yixiao Chen, Lei Xin, Jianbo Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Induction circuits retrieve a value by matching a query to information carried from the token before that value. How this address forms, incorporates context, and supports sequential retrieval remains less clear. We use hybrid language models as a tractable testbed and identify the historical source key as a common causal interface between local preparation and global retrieval. Probes and restoration interventions connect this interface across controlled models, pretrained recurrent and sliding-window hybrids, and released language models. We organize the analysis by address context radius and sequential retrieval depth. First, shared local parameters couple predecessor Carrying to recurrent state processing; separating these demands or biasing initialization promotes address formation. Second, adapting input representations extends an established literal circuit to category matching, making semantically compatible records active competitors. Earlier context resolves this competition through the source key, with a sharp visibility boundary in a controlled single-read model and compositional relays across local layers. Third, fixed relations support memorization shortcuts, whereas sequential generalization requires learning an output-to-query bridge between retrievals. Controlled state-tracking and compositional in-context tasks demonstrate this requirement across stacked stages, looped recurrence, and intermediate-token generation. Together, these results connect circuit formation, contextual disambiguation, and sequential use through a shared retrieval interface. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DynSHAP: Towards Explainable Dynamic Survival Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13042)**  `arXiv:2609.13042`  
  _Nastasya Anokhina, Jonas Jürß, Pietro Liò_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis (DSA) achieve strong predictive performance by incorporating longitudinal patient data, but their black box nature limits clinical trust and adoption. Existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously, which limits their usability in DSA. We propose DynSHAP, a SHAP framework suited specifically for dynamic survival analysis. It extends common marginal SHAP estimators to this setting by treating time--feature pairs as players in the Shapley game. We further introduce Temporal DynSHAP, which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address them in explanations. When applied to synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators for a given state-of-the-art model. Applied to two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces attributions faithful to model learning, allowing medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when.
  </details>

- **[LeanStream: A Speculate-and-Refine Streaming Framework for Efficient on-Device LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03079)**  `arXiv:2609.03079`  
  _Renyuan Liu, Yuyang Leng, Kaiyan Liu, Yuzhou Zhong, Shaohan Hu, Chun-Fu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-device LLM inference is attractive for privacy and responsiveness, but remains challenging on mobile and embedded devices because model weights far exceed available DRAM. Prior systems exploit activation sparsity and offload weights to SSD or flash storage, but face a fundamental systems trade-off: accurate sparse execution decisions require the latest context, whereas efficient computation-I/O overlap requires early prediction. As a result, existing designs either serialize execution or incur redundant weight fetches, extra computation, and large cache overheads. We present LeanStream, a streaming speculate-and-refine framework for efficient on-device LLM inference. LeanStream progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results, enabling fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O. We implement LeanStream on both mobile and embedded platforms. Compared with prior on-device LLM inference systems, LeanStream reduces memory usage by 4.8$\times$ to 7.5$\times$ at the best throughput achieved by prior work, while further improving token generation throughput by 1.6$\times$ to 2.1$\times$. Source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[More accurate behavioral predictions with hybrid Bayesian-connectionist models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22154)**  `arXiv:2608.22154`  
  _Brenden M. Lake, Akshay K. Jagadish, Guangyuan Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Researchers must often choose between Bayesian or neural network models of behavior, two paradigms with complementary strengths and weaknesses. An ideal paradigm would facilitate testing many kinds of representations and inductive biases; Bayesian models make this easy, while neural networks do not. Similarly, an ideal paradigm would avoid over-simplifications; neural networks make this easy, while Bayesian models do not. Here, we introduce Bayesian distillation with Behavioral Tuning (BBT) as an approach to getting the best of both traditions. BBT offers a simple recipe for model building: first, a neural network is trained to mimic a Bayesian model through synthetic data, and second, the network is fine-tuned on human behavior to capture additional structure and nuance. Across four case studies in human concept learning, we find that BBT outperforms traditional approaches at predicting human behavior while also revealing psychological insights, resulting in models that can both mimic Bayesian priors and capture heuristics and biases that violate simple modeling assumptions.
  </details>

- **[FAR-DPO: Feasibility-Aware and Robust Direct Preference Optimization for Cyclic Peptide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19808)**  `arXiv:2608.19808`  
  _Guofeng Zhang, Rong Han, Xiaoyu Wang, Zhiyun Li, Zongbo Han, Xiaohong Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cyclic peptides are emerging as promising molecular scaffolds in drug discovery due to their high binding affinity and structural stability. However, extending generative models from linear to cyclic peptide design remains challenging, as cyclization sharply restricts the feasible design space through coupled geometric and biophysical constraints. Moreover, limited training data has led existing approaches to rely largely on zero-shot generation or post hoc filtering, resulting in low yields of feasible designs and limited control over multi-objective trade-offs. To address these limitations, we propose FAR-DPO (Feasibility-Aware and Robust Direct Preference Optimization), an architecture-agnostic framework that steers generative models toward structurally and biophysically feasible cyclic peptide designs, particularly for challenging targets. FAR-DPO integrates feasibility-aware preference construction with difficulty-aware group-robust optimization. Specifically, it constructs within-target preference pairs through feasibility-gated multi-objective dominance and adaptively reweights predefined difficulty groups according to their current preference losses. Under a fixed generation budget, FAR-DPO increases overall success rate on the CPSea benchmark from 46.9% to 57.8% on PepGLAD and from 48.0% to 49.6% on PepFlow. These gains also extend to the hardest target quartile and are accompanied by more favorable best-per-target binding scores. Together, these results demonstrate FAR-DPO's effectiveness in improving feasibility and target-wise robustness.
  </details>

- **[A Locally Tokenized Generative Model for Robust Time-Series Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19727)**  `arXiv:2608.19727`  
  _Dongbin Kim, Geonwoo Shin, Yujin Choi, Soyeon Park, Jaewook Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Watermarking is a central tool for provenance in generative models, yet its application to multivariate time series remains hindered by reliability failures under post-editing attacks. We show that existing detectors, which rely on globally coupled re-encoding, suffer from bidirectional drift of the null distribution: post-editing attacks can shift the $z$-score of non-watermarked samples in either direction, invalidating clean-calibrated thresholds. We argue that this instability is a property of the re-encoding, and that reliable detection requires each recovered unit to depend only on a bounded temporal neighborhood. Guided by this principle, we propose L-VQVAE, a generative model in which each discrete token is produced from a short contiguous window, and LVQMark, a watermarking method over this token space that combines logit-bias injection with robust re-encoding for attack-time detection. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning finance, energy, and neuroimaging show that our approach preserves generation quality while stabilizing both detection power and false-positive behavior under post-editing attacks.
  </details>

- **[Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow for Few-Step Generative Adaptation from Any Parameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19540)**  `arXiv:2608.19540`  
  _Yara Bahram, Zahra Dehghani, Mélodie Desbos, Eric Granger, Pablo Piantanida, Mohammadhadi Shateri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training fast generators on new domains with limited data remains challenging for two reasons. First, adapting a pretrained diffusion or flow model leaves its costly multi-step sampling intact, and existing acceleration methods are tied to the source parameterization (x, epsilon, v, or u), so heterogeneous pretrained models share no common acceleration target. Second, while adversarial post-training sharpens generation quality, existing criteria each cover only one end of the step range. Discrete criteria compare jump endpoints and lose their signal as the jump shrinks, while continuous criteria score the instantaneous velocity and misjudge the large jumps of few-step sampling. Neither fits MeanFlow (MF) models, which predict average velocities over intervals of any length. We propose MeanFlow-Transfer (MF-T), which adapts a source domain model of any parameterization and compresses it into a few-step MF generator through a common velocity map. We then introduce Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow (CAMF), which scores the change of a learned potential along each predicted interval jump of MF. CAMF needs no gradient penalty or Jacobian-vector product (JVP). We also prove it stays well-conditioned for every interval length and recovers the instantaneous criterion as the interval vanishes. Adapting four ImageNet-based source models (DiT, SiT, JiT, iMF) to five target domains, MF-T + CAMF approaches or exceeds the fine-tuned many-step teacher in FID and FDD at up to 125x fewer Neural Function Evaluations (NFEs). Further, CAMF improves MF-T's few-step FID by about 30% on average, outperforming prior adversarial post-training on iMF at every NFE.
  </details>

- **[CytoBERT: A Foundation Model for Cytometry Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14414)**  `arXiv:2608.14414`  
  _Syed Abdul Haseeb Qadri, Bjarne C. Hiller, Felix Blanke, Vanja Sophie Cangalovic, Kutalmış Coşkun, Amin Mirzaei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cytometry measures the complex characteristics of single cells (e.g., counts and protein expression of immune cells) and is widely used across immunological research and clinical settings. However, cytometry data is highly heterogeneous and unstandardized due to experimental protocols and the choice of measured features. While machine learning methods hold the potential to gain deeper insights into cell biology, these challenges make them difficult to apply and transfer across studies. Recent advances in foundation models can alleviate these issues, but corresponding approaches are still scarce in this field. To address this, we provide CytoBERT, a publicly available, open-source, open-weight foundation model for single-cell cytometry data with variable marker panels. CytoBERT is pretrained in a self-supervised manner on a large-scale cytometry corpus (15 human datasets with heterogeneous marker panels and more than 50 million cells) curated through marker standardization, enabling it to learn transferable inter-marker relationships within cells. Fine-tuning CytoBERT for sample-level classification demonstrates that transfer learning across heterogeneous cytometry datasets is feasible, providing a starting point for scalable, generalizable cytometry analysis. Code is available at GitHub.
  </details>

- **[Variable-aware representation, not relation typing, explains the performance of an event-graph model for ICU mortality prediction: temporal and external validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02663)**  `arXiv:2608.02663`  
  _Mohammad Nasir Uddin, Eklachur Rahman Bhuiyan, Rahnuma Tabassum Orpita_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event-graph neural networks represent intensive care unit (ICU) records as typed entities linked by timestamped observations, and their gains are often attributed to relation-specific message passing. We tested which components matter once leakage is removed and validation is temporal and external. In 31,142 first ICU stays of at least 48 hours in the Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care IV (MIMIC-IV), models predicting post-48-hour in-hospital mortality were developed on 2008-2016 stays, tested on held-out 2008-2016 stays and on 2017-2019 and 2020-2022 stays, and applied without retraining to 54,696 stays from 205 hospitals in the eICU Collaborative Research Database. A leak in a previous version, total ICU length of stay used as an input, was removed. A variable-aware representation (a learned embedding of each node's variable plus per-variable normalisation) raised the mean single-model area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) from 0.846 to 0.896 in 2020-2022 and from 0.699 to 0.809 externally. With it, relation-specific and shared (relation-collapsed) message passing differed by less than 0.005 AUROC in every test set, within a +/-0.01 equivalence margin, although relation typing had appeared beneficial without it. The revised model's AUROC was 0.878-0.900 across MIMIC-IV periods, 0.15-0.19 above three severity scores and 0.007-0.011 below gradient boosting. Externally, the five-model average reached AUROC 0.825 versus 0.788 for the Acute Physiology and Chronic Health Evaluation (APACHE) IVa score on the 48,184 stays with an APACHE prediction. In this model, representation mattered more than message-passing architecture.
  </details>

- **[TabPFN beyond Tabular Data: Calibration and Accuracy on Multimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11007)**  `arXiv:2607.11007`  
  _Jingxiang Zhang, Lujia Zhong, Zijie Zhu, Shuo Huang, Yuang Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot multimodal classification commonly attaches a lightweight head, such as $k$-nearest neighbors, logistic regression, or a linear SVM, to a frozen pretrained encoder. Although computationally efficient, these heads can produce poorly calibrated confidence scores. We ask whether TabPFN can provide reliable confidence estimates on multimodal embeddings without sacrificing predictive accuracy, and under what conditions. We systematically evaluate TabPFN as a zero-gradient head for frozen image, text, and audio encoders. Across 22{,}820 evaluation episodes spanning 14 datasets, 11 encoders, and three modalities, TabPFN achieves the best mean rank among nine classification heads on both negative log-likelihood (NLL) and expected calibration error (ECE). At a representative setting, it reduces NLL by 48--62\% and ECE by 2.1--5.3$\times$ relative to the average of eight baselines while matching or exceeding their average accuracy. This calibration benefit transfers broadly, whereas the accuracy advantage is conditional: it concentrates at moderate-to-high shot counts and low-to-moderate feature dimensions ($k \ge 50$, $d \le 32$), and diminishes when labeled data are scarce, feature dimensions are high, or competing methods approach ceiling accuracy. After backbone adaptation, replacing the trained linear head with TabPFN improves calibration while preserving competitive accuracy, showing that representation adaptation and reliable head choice are complementary. Together, these results identify when TabPFN can serve as a training-free head for calibration-sensitive multimodal classification. To support transparency and reproducibility, we publicly release the source code, experiment configurations, and evaluation scripts in our GitHub repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[RL Forgets! Towards Continual Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04364)**  `arXiv:2607.04364`  
  _Mao-Lin Luo, Zhe-Xu Wang, Zi-Hao Zhou, Bo Ye, Jian Zhao, Min-Ling Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual post-training is becoming a central paradigm for adapting vision-language models to evolving tasks. Recent studies report that reinforcement learning is less prone to forgetting than supervised fine-tuning, motivating the view that RL is inherently resistant to forgetting. However, this view remains insufficiently validated, as existing evidence is largely drawn from outdated or homogeneous benchmarks. We revisit this assumption by introducing MRCL, a Multimodal Reasoning Continual Learning benchmark built from recent and diverse multimodal reasoning tasks. Experiments on MRCL show that standard reinforcement learning still suffers from catastrophic forgetting during continual post-training. We trace the failure to an objective mismatch. The KL regularization used in common policy optimization methods is evaluated on current-task data, whereas forgetting is caused by behavioral drift on prior-task distributions. To address this problem, we propose Continual Policy Optimization (CPO), a replay-free method grounded in a prior-task behavioral KL objective. CPO derives a local Fisher surrogate from the historical KL objective and uses parameter movement as a gradient-free proxy for Fisher sensitivity, enabling sparse regularization with negligible additional overhead. Experiments on three model scales and comparisons with multiple RL baselines show that CPO consistently reduces forgetting while maintaining effective adaptation and preserving broader pretrained capabilities. The implementation code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Explicit 3D Gaussian Models for Fast, Mesh-Free Compression of Structured and Unstructured Volumes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01164)**  `arXiv:2607.01164`  
  _Landon Dyken, Sharmistha Chakrabarti, Nathan Debardeleben, Steve Petruzza, Qi Wu, Will Usher, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has shown that implicit neural representations (INRs) can be trained to effectively compress structured and unstructured volume data, allowing for direct data querying with a reduced memory footprint. However, as existing INRs for unstructured volumes do not encode geometry, they require partial mesh storage for later sampling, limiting achievable compression. At the same time, novel view synthesis methods have shown that explicit collections of 3D Gaussians can be used to accurately visualize volume data. In this work, we introduce an explicit model for volume data compression based on 3D Gaussian primitives. We reinterpret collections of 3D Gaussians as an explicit representation of a volume's scalar field and use a sampling strategy that reconstructs scalar values at spatial locations through weighted aggregation of intersecting Gaussians.
